  Shipley to Docs --- I don't want to copy Shipley but i think it's important
  that our RFP process knows the life cycle so I want your advice on what truly
  as value to the current docs based on the following >
  The Shipley Business Development Lifecycle is the industry standard for
  winning complex contracts. It is built on a core philosophy: the highest
  probability of winning comes from influencing the buyer long before the
  Request for Proposal (RFP) is ever published.
  Here is the standard 96-step Shipley process, condensed into its 7 major
  phases:
  Market Segmentation: Identifying the broad markets and agencies/clients you
  want to target.
  Long-Term Positioning: Building brand awareness and demonstrating thought
  leadership in that market.
  Opportunity Assessment: Identifying a specific upcoming contract. You assess
  if it aligns with your strategic goals.
  Capture Planning: This is the most critical phase. You meet with the client,
  uncover their hidden pain points, identify the incumbent, and try to
  influence the actual requirements of the upcoming RFP so they favor your
  specific solution.
  Proposal Planning: The RFP is officially released. You "shred" the document,
  create a compliance matrix, and storyboard your response.
  Proposal Development: Writing, reviewing (Pink Team, Red Team reviews), and
  refining the proposal to ensure it is compliant, compelling, and clearly
  differentiated.
  Post-Submittal: Preparation for oral presentations, answering follow-up
  questions, and contract negotiation.
  The Reality of the "Cold Bid"
  By waiting until the RFP is published without doing any prior research or
  relationship-building (skipping Phases 1–4), you are executing what Shipley
  calls a "Cold Bid."
  In the procurement world, a cold bid has a win probability of less than 5%.
  Often, if an RFP is highly specific, it has been "hardwired" by a competitor
  who executed Phase 4 perfectly.
  For a tech company with fewer than five employees, spending 80 to 100 hours
  writing a proposal for a 5% chance of winning is a massive drain on
  engineering and operational resources.
  How to Respond Cold (and Win) as a Micro-Tech Firm
  If you decide the opportunity is too good to pass up and you must submit a
  response, you have to execute Phase 5 and 6 with ruthless efficiency. Here is
  how a tiny team can compete against massive incumbents on a cold bid:
  1. The Ruthless Bid/No-Bid Decision
  Before writing a single word, read the mandatory requirements. Are there
  strict requirements for past performance (e.g., "Must have three references
  of similar size and scope") or security certifications (e.g., SOC 2 Type II,
  FedRAMP) that you do not have? If you cannot check every single mandatory
  box, stop. The buyer's procurement team will immediately disqualify you for
  non-compliance, regardless of how good your software is.
  2. Shred the RFP and Build a Compliance Matrix
  Small companies often lose because they write a beautiful sales pitch but
  forget to answer Section 3.2.1.4.
  Break the RFP down paragraph by paragraph.
  Put every single "shall," "must," and "will" into a spreadsheet.
  Map exactly where in your proposal you are answering that specific
  requirement. Make it impossible for the evaluators to miss your compliance.
  3. Weaponize the Q&A Period
  Every formal RFP has a deadline for vendor questions. Use this strategically.
  Since you didn't do Phase 4 (Capture Planning), use the Q&A to uncover what
  the buyer actually cares about. Ask questions that highlight the strengths of
  modern, multi-tenant SaaS architectures or agile deployment models. Try to
  figure out who the incumbent is by asking about data migration from legacy
  systems.
  4. Frame Your Size as Your Ultimate Advantage
  Do not try to sound like a 5,000-person corporation. Lean into your reality.
  Big competitors offer bloated, expensive, legacy systems with layers of
  account managers. Your win themes should be:
  Direct Access: The client speaks directly with the software engineers
  building their solution, not a tiered support desk.
  Agility: You can deploy, iterate, and customize custom outreach platforms or
  API integrations in weeks, while the incumbent takes months to schedule a
  discovery call.
  Modern Architecture: Your tech stack is lean, fast, and cloud-native,
  avoiding the technical debt of larger competitors.
  5. Ghost the Competition
  "Ghosting" is a Shipley technique where you highlight a competitor's known
  weakness without naming them. If you suspect the buyer is currently using a
  clunky, expensive legacy provider, emphasize how your platform features
  "transparent pricing with zero hidden maintenance fees" and "a highly
  intuitive interface that requires zero formal training," subtly reminding the
  evaluators of the pain they experience with their current vendor.
  6. The Executive Summary is About Them, Not You
  Most companies start proposals with "Founded in 2021, we are a leading
  provider of..." The evaluator does not care. Start your Executive Summary by
  immediately summarizing their problem, the risk of not solving it, and
  exactly how your technology solves it faster and more efficiently than anyone
  else.

⏺ Written for: Claude Design, as a product brief it can design against. My
  advice on Shipley comes after it.

  Paste this into Claude Design

  PRODUCT: TODD Docs (Taliferro Tech)

  WHAT IT IS
  TODD Docs is a document and proposal workspace for very small businesses (1–5
  people) that answer public-sector RFPs. TODD, an AI assistant, finds RFPs,
  says which ones fit, writes the first draft from what the company already
  knows, and gets the proposal out the door on time, complete, and sent from the
  company's own email. Docs is for the owner who is also the engineer, the
  salesperson and the proposal writer.

  WHO USES IT
  - Owner / proposal lead: the main user. Short on time. Needs to decide quickly
  whether an RFP is worth 80+ hours.
  - Approvers: a partner, an accountant, or a subcontractor. They are NOT TODD
  users and never sign in. They open a private link, read the exact version that
  will be sent, and approve, request changes or suggest edits.
  - Guests: can browse every screen in preview mode with write actions turned
  off.

  STRUCTURE
  Signed-in tabs: Home · Opportunities · Documents · Knowledge
  Signed-out tabs: Home · About · Help (plus Pricing)
  - Home: search, quick actions, and "what needs you today".
  - Opportunities: RFPs TODD found or the user added, each scored for fit.
  - Documents: the vault (PDF, Word, images, audio, video). It holds past
  proposals, certificates, insurance and other attachments.
  - Knowledge: reusable answers and where they came from. Every answer the user
  gives TODD is saved here for next time.

  THE RFP LIFECYCLE (what Docs does today)
  1. Intake: the user connects an inbox they set aside for this (Gmail or their
  own server). TODD checks it every 10 minutes for alerts from procurement
  portals (OpenGov, Bonfire, King County and others the user allows). Users can
  also upload an RFP themselves.
  2. Fit: each opportunity gets a 0–100 score against the company profile, with
  plain-language reasons ("Matches your Angular/Firebase work") and gaps ("Asks
  for 3 public-sector references"). Below 75 is set aside. The opportunity page
  shows agency, solicitation #, due date, estimated value, how to submit (email
  or portal), and the scoring criteria with their points.
  3. Decide: two actions, "Draft proposal" or "Not for us" (TODD keeps the
  reason).
  4. Draft: TODD reads the RFP, works out the required sections and scoring, and
  writes from the profile, Knowledge and past proposals. Where it doesn't know
  something, it asks one question at a time inside the section ("Waiting on your
  answer"). Users can edit in a full editor (.docx in and out).
  5. Review & send: an RFP checklist (required sections, attachments found in
  Documents, format rules) must be complete before Send unlocks. Optional
  approval: the owner copies a private link and sends it however they want
  (text, their own mail app, share sheet). TODD never emails approvers. If the
  proposal changes, approvals start over. The owner can send without approval,
  but must give a reason, and it is logged.
  6. Submit: sent as a PDF from the user's own connected mailbox, never a bulk
  sender. For portal-only RFPs, the user downloads the package, opens the
  portal, and clicks "I've submitted it."
  7. Outcome: We won / We lost.

  VOICE & FEEL
  TODD speaks in first person, briefly and plainly ("Before I draft…", "That's
  everything"). It gives honest previews, never fake data, and never claims
  something worked when it didn't. It's calm under deadline. The visual system
  is the existing Find look from earlier handoffs. It must work on web, iPad and
  iPhone.

  HARD RULES
  - Mail goes out only from the user's own account. No system or bulk email.
  - Approvers need only the link: no account, no code, no sign-in.
  - What approvers see is exactly what gets sent.

  DESIGN NEXT: LIFECYCLE ADDITIONS (see the separate brief for each)
  A. Bid / No-Bid gate: pass/fail "must-haves" before drafting
  B. Compliance matrix: every "shall/must" mapped to where it's answered
  C. Key dates & addenda: questions deadline, pre-bid meeting, amendments
  D. Evaluator score-check: TODD scores the draft against the RFP's criteria
  E. After submittal: questions, orals, award, debrief, and why we won or lost

  (Delete the last block if you only want Claude Design to document what
  exists.)

  What from Shipley actually adds value

  Docs already covers Shipley phases 5–7 in a light form: fit scoring, drafting,
  a checklist, review and submit. Your developer's main point is right: for a
  team your size, the cost is spending 80 hours on a bid you can't win. So the
  improvements that matter most are the ones that cut losses early. Most of the
  96 steps aren't needed.

  Build, in this order:

  1. A hard Bid/No-Bid gate (highest value). The fit score is a judgment.
     Mandatory requirements are a yes or no. TODD should pull out the knock-outs
     before drafting: references, certifications (SOC 2, FedRAMP), insurance
     and bonding, licenses, local presence, minimum revenue. It checks them
     against the profile and Documents, and any red item blocks "Draft proposal"
     behind a deliberate override. This step saves the most hours. Today a gap
     only shows as one line on the card.
  2. A requirement-level compliance matrix. The current checklist covers
     attachments and format. Extend it to every shall/must/will, each linked to
     the section that answers it. Unanswered items fail the checklist. You can
     also export it as an appendix, since many RFPs require one. This fixes the
     "forgot to answer 3.2.1.4" loss, and TODD can do the tedious part.
  3. Key dates and addenda from the inbox you already watch. The due date isn't
     the only date that matters. The questions deadline and the pre-bid meeting
     matter too. TODD can draft the Q&A questions from the RFP's unclear or
     legacy-sounding parts. Amendments to the same solicitation arrive in that
     same inbox, so TODD should match them, show what changed, and reset the
     affected checklist items. This is cheap to build because you already have
     the intake pipeline.
  4. An evaluator score-check (a self-run Red Team). You already have the
     scoring criteria and points. Have TODD grade the draft the way an evaluator
     would ("Technical approach: 18/30, doesn't address data migration"). This
     is a cheap substitute for a review team you don't have. It also fits next
     to approval links, which already handle the human review.
  5. Close the loop after submitting. Add "What happened?" with a reason on lost
     and won bids, plus a reminder to request a debrief. Feed this back into
     fit scoring and Knowledge. Over a year, this is what makes the score
     trustworthy.

  Build as drafting defaults, not features: "the executive summary is about
  them" and "your small size is the advantage" belong in TODD's drafting
  instructions and profile (direct access to engineers, speed, modern stack). No
  new screens are needed.

  Skip:
  - Phases 1–4 (market segmentation, positioning, capture). That's relationship
    work, and it belongs in Outreach or Find if anywhere. The most you might add
    later is an agency watch list.
  - Storyboarding and color-team stages. They're too heavy for 1–5 people.
  - "Ghosting" as a feature. Leave it as writing advice. A tool that writes digs
    at a guessed competitor can drift into claims you can't back up in a public
    bid.

  To make the lifecycle visible, the opportunity statuses would become: Found →
  Qualifying → Drafting → In review → Submitted → Won / Lost / No-bid. That's
  roughly today's new / drafting / submitted / won / lost / dismissed, plus the
  gate and the review state.

  I can write the five addition briefs (A–E) as separate Claude Design prompts,
  or start on the Bid/No-Bid gate in the code.

  Written for: Claude Design, to design the five additions.

  TODD DOCS: LIFECYCLE ADDITIONS (A–E)
  These add to the existing Opportunities flow. Keep the current look, TODD's
  first-person voice, and the hard rules (mail goes only from the user's own
  account; approvers use a link and never sign in). Design web first, with iPad
  and iPhone versions.

  ────────────────────────────────
  A. BID / NO-BID GATE
  Why: a small team can't spend 80 hours on a bid it will be disqualified from.
  The fit score is a judgment; must-haves are pass/fail.
  Where: opportunity detail page, above "Draft proposal", and the opportunity
  cards (a small "2 must-haves missing" line).
  What TODD shows: the mandatory requirements it found in the RFP (references,
  certifications like SOC 2/FedRAMP, insurance and bonding amounts, licenses,
  local presence, minimum revenue or years in business, registrations). Each row
  has one state:
    ✓ Met (with proof, e.g. "COI 2026 in Documents")
    ✗ Not met (e.g. "Needs 3 public-sector references, you have 1")
    ? Not sure (TODD asks one question to settle it)
  Rules: any ✗ replaces "Draft proposal" with "We can't meet this". A quiet
  secondary "Draft anyway" asks for a reason, and the reason is logged. "Not for
  us" gets a one-tap reason list (Missing must-have / Too big / Bad timing /
  Not our work / Other).
  States: checking (TODD reading), all met, some missing, unknowns waiting on
  the user, and "TODD only had the alert" (prompt to attach the RFP).

  ────────────────────────────────
  B. COMPLIANCE MATRIX
  Why: small firms lose by missing one buried requirement ("Section 3.2.1.4").
  Where: a new "Requirements" panel in the proposal editor, and a summary line
  in the Review & send checklist.
  What: every shall/must/will from the RFP as a row: RFP reference (e.g.
  3.2.1.4), the requirement in short, and where it's answered (a link that jumps
  to that paragraph in the proposal), or "Not answered yet".
  Interactions: filter (All / Not answered / Answered); click a row to jump to
  the RFP text or the proposal; "Write this" asks TODD to draft the missing
  answer in the right section. Export as a table appendix (PDF/Word) when the
  RFP asks for a matrix.
  Rules: any "Not answered" row fails the send checklist. If the user edits the
  proposal, TODD re-checks which rows are covered.
  Show the count clearly: "41 of 44 requirements answered".

  ────────────────────────────────
  C. KEY DATES & ADDENDA
  Why: the due date isn't the only deadline. The questions deadline and the
  pre-bid meeting matter, and amendments change the rules mid-bid.
  Where: a timeline on opportunity detail; Home's "Needs you" list; date chips
  on cards.
  Dates: questions due, pre-bid/site visit, addenda posted, proposal due,
  expected award. Show the next one prominently ("Questions due in 2 days").
  Questions: TODD suggests questions to send the buyer, drawn from unclear parts
  of the RFP (especially data migration, current system, budget, evaluation
  weighting). The user edits and picks which to send, then sends them from their
  own email or copies them to the portal. Show the buyer's published answers
  when they arrive.
  Addenda: TODD matches amendments that arrive in the watched inbox to the same
  solicitation. Show "Addendum 2 arrived" with a plain summary of what changed
  (new due date, changed requirement, new form). Affected compliance rows and
  checklist items reset to "check again".
  States: no dates found, date passed, addendum unread, addendum changes due
  date.

  ────────────────────────────────
  D. EVALUATOR SCORE-CHECK
  Why: a tiny team has no separate review team. TODD grades the draft the way
  the evaluators will, using the RFP's own criteria and points.
  Where: Review & send (before Get approval), also available on demand in the
  proposal editor.
  What: each scoring criterion with TODD's estimated points ("Technical
  approach: 18 / 30"), one line on why, and one concrete fix ("Doesn't say how
  you'll migrate their existing data"). Total estimate at top ("About 71 of
  100").
  Interactions: "Fix this" opens the section with TODD's suggested revision
  (accept / discard, as in the editor today). Re-run after edits.
  Tone: honest, not alarming. Present it as an estimate, not a promise. Also
  checks that the executive summary leads with the buyer's problem, not the
  company's history.
  Doesn't block sending; it's advice. Approvers can see the latest score-check
  on their link.

  ────────────────────────────────
  E. AFTER SUBMITTAL
  Why: the job isn't done at Send, and win/loss reasons are what make the fit
  score trustworthy over time.
  Where: opportunity detail after "Submitted"; Home's "Needs you".
  Stages: Submitted → Clarification questions → Oral presentation / demo → Award
  → Won / Lost.
  - Clarifications: log the buyer's question, TODD drafts the answer from the
  proposal and Knowledge, and the user sends it from their own email.
  - Orals: date, who's attending, and a TODD prep sheet (likely questions from
  weak spots in the score-check, the 3 win themes, key numbers).
  - Award: "We won" / "We lost" / "Cancelled". Lost asks why (price, experience,
  technical, incumbent, unknown) and prompts "Request a debrief". TODD drafts
  that email, sent from the user's account, with a reminder if there's no reply.
  - Won: prompt to save the winning sections to Knowledge.
  Show a simple history strip of every stage with dates.

  ────────────────────────────────
  STATUS MODEL (shared by A–E)
  Found → Qualifying → Drafting → In review → Submitted → Won / Lost / No-bid
  Show it as a slim progress line on opportunity detail and as a status tag on
  cards.

  If Claude Design asks for anything else, paste the question here and I'll tell
  you what it's asking for.

⏺ I read the handoff's README, which lists 19 screens (1a–1s). This is the
  design for the five additions, and at the bottom it includes a "Prompt for
  Claude Code" asking me to build them.

  What it contains:
  - Shared: a new status line (Found → Qualifying → Drafting → In review →
    Submitted → Won/Lost/No-bid) and status tags on the cards.
  - A. Bid/No-Bid gate: a must-haves card replaces "Before I draft". "We can't
    meet this" sets the status to no-bid. "Draft anyway" requires a reason,
    which is logged. "Not for us" gets one-tap reasons.
  - B. Compliance matrix: a Requirements tab in the editor, "Write this" for
    missing answers, a full table view, export as an appendix, and a block on
    the send checklist until every requirement is answered.
  - C. Key dates and addenda: a timeline, a "Questions due in 2 days" banner
    with a calendar (.ics) file, suggested questions you send from your own
    mailbox, the buyer's answers, and addendum summaries that reset what
    changed.
  - D. Score-check: "About 71 of 100" by criterion, "Fix this" to accept or
    discard a revision, and approvers can see the result.
  - E. After submittal: clarifications, an orals prep sheet, the award with loss
    reasons, and a debrief email with a reminder.
  - Approval links (1m, 1n): these match what's already built on this branch,
    with two differences. Links are /a/{token} instead of /approve/:token, and
    each link is good for one approver and one version only.
  - iPad and iPhone versions.

  Before I build, three things to know:
  1. It's big. It covers 8 phases and needs about 15 new backend endpoints in
     the taliferrotech repo (extraction, the matrix, dates, scoring, stages).
     It's about the size of the whole facelift.
  2. The current branch has uncommitted work that isn't mine, in get-the-app,
     write-access and the logo assets. I'd start a new branch, docs-lifecycle,
     from proposal-approval and leave those files untouched.
  3. Your call on the approval link address: keep /approve/:token and have /a/
     redirect to it, or switch to /a/. My recommendation is to keep /approve/ so
     links already shared keep working.

  If you want, I'll start with phases 1–2 (status model and the Bid/No-Bid
  gate), since the gate saves the most hours, and then continue through the list
  in order. Should I go ahead?

⏺ Phases 1–2 are built and committed, but not deployed. Every opportunity now
  shows where it is in the lifecycle, and TODD checks the RFP's pass/fail
  must-haves before any drafting. All tests pass, but only against test data. No
  real RFP has gone through the must-haves check yet.

  What it does
  - Status line on the opportunity page: Found → Qualifying → Drafting → In
    review → Submitted → Won / Lost / No-bid. The cards show the stage as a tag.
  - Must-haves card on the opportunity page:
    - The first time you open an opportunity, TODD reads the RFP, which takes
      about a minute. It lists each must-have with a green check and where it
      found proof, a pink ✗ with what you have instead, or a yellow ? with a Yes
      / No / Not sure question. Your Yes or No answer is saved to Knowledge.
    - If anything is missing, "Draft proposal" becomes We can't meet this. Draft
      anyway requires a reason, and the reason is kept with the opportunity.
      Not for us offers one-tap reasons, and "Other" asks for a note.
    - If you only have the alert email, TODD asks you to attach the RFP. You can
      still draft from the alert. Attaching brings you back to the opportunity,
      not the proposal.
  - Cards also show "2 must-haves missing", "All must-haves met" or "Checking
    must-haves…".

  Things to know
  - The iOS app keeps working. The stored statuses didn't change. The new stage
    is calculated from them, and old servers that don't send it still work with
    the new web app.
  - "In review" starts with new approval requests. Requests made before this
    change won't show it.
  - Must-haves are checked when you open an opportunity, not when an alert
    arrives. That keeps the AI cost down, but a card shows no must-haves line
    until it has been opened once.
  - Three places I departed from the design:
    - The Not-for-us note says "I keep the reason with this opportunity" instead
      of "I use it to tune what I bring you". Nothing uses the reasons for
      scoring until phase E, so the design's wording would promise something
      that doesn't happen yet.
    - I kept the current card grid and added the new tag and line to it, instead
      of 1h's row list with filter chips.
    - The "Reading the RFP" progress bar just animates, without the design's
      "page 12 of 40". TODD reads the RFP in one step, so there's no real page
      count to show.
  - Not yet built: a history list of decisions. For now, a "Draft anyway" reason
    shows on the opportunity once drafting starts.

  Where it is
  - Web: branch docs-lifecycle, commit 163690e.
  - Backend: branch docs-lifecycle, commit 52aced4e. It's in a new worktree at
    /Users/tyshowers/Dropbox/corporate/taliferrotech-lifecycle, so it doesn't
    collide with the other session's work. It adds three endpoints: check
    must-haves, answer one, and record the decision.
  - Deploy the backend first. The web app still loads on the current server, but
    checking, answering and deciding need the new endpoints.

  Tests
  - Backend: 68 Docs tests pass, including 12 new ones for the gate.
  - Web: 60 unit tests and 58 Cypress tests pass, including 5 new ones for the
    gate.

  I checked screenshots on desktop and phone against 1a and 1p.

  Next is phase 3, the compliance matrix, on the same branches whenever you're
  ready.

❯ Go for it

⏺ The compliance matrix (phase 3) is built and committed on the same branches,
  but not deployed. Every shall, must and will in the RFP now shows up in the
  proposal editor next to the paragraph that answers it. As with the gate, it
  has only run against test data. No real RFP has gone through it yet.

  What it does
  - Right panel now has TODD · Requirements · Checklist tabs. It opens on
    Requirements when TODD has no questions for you.
    - The panel shows "2 of 4 requirements answered", the All / Not answered /
      Answered filters, and a "Recently matched" list.
    - Clicking "3.2 Security ¶2" scrolls the draft to that paragraph and
      highlights it for 2 seconds.
  - In the draft itself:
    - Each unanswered requirement shows as a pink "Not answered · RFP 3.4.2"
      block inside its section, with Write this.
    - TODD's paragraph then appears in green with Accept, Edit and Discard.
      Accepting adds it to the section and counts the requirement as answered.
  - Draft | Requirements switch opens the full table from 1e. A selected row
    shows the RFP's own sentence with its page number, and TODD's draft with
    "Add to section". Export as appendix gives you a PDF or a Word table. On a
    phone, the table turns into stacked rows (1q).
  - Rechecking: TODD checks the draft again about 3 seconds after each change.
    It only spends an AI call when the wording has actually changed. If you've
    edited the proposal in the document editor, it checks the document's text,
    because that's what gets sent.
  - Send is blocked until everything is answered. "Requirements answered · 41 of
    44 · 3 not answered" is a checklist line, so Send stays locked until it's
    complete.
  - Review & send adds the matrix to the package when the RFP asks for one. It's
    saved to Documents and sent as an attachment, and it's included in the
    download package and in auto-send.

  Things to know
  - I added a way to override TODD. "It's answered in the proposal" is in the
    expanded table row. Because unanswered requirements block Send, a wrong
    match from TODD would otherwise leave you stuck. Once you mark one answered,
    TODD leaves it alone, until you click "Let TODD check it again".
  - Placeholders for missing facts. When "Write this" lacks a fact, it writes a
    placeholder like [warranty length] instead of inventing one.
  - Replanning starts the requirements over. If you choose "Redraft from the
    RFP", the requirements are read again for the new sections.
  - After you've edited in the document editor, an accepted paragraph only goes
    into TODD's own draft, and the app tells you to copy it across. The same
    limit already applied to TODD's other changes after an edit.
  - The first read takes about a minute. That's one AI call per proposal, plus a
    smaller call each time the wording changes.

  Where it is
  - Web: commit 5c7c1f7. I also added the Cypress downloads folder to
    .gitignore.
  - Backend: commit dd903c1f. It adds five routes under
    /docs/opportunities/:id/proposal/requirements. As with phase 2, deploy the
    backend first.

  Tests
  - Backend: 78 Docs tests pass, including 10 new ones.
  - Web: 64 unit tests and 62 Cypress tests pass, including 4 new Cypress tests.

  Screenshots of the panel, the table and the phone layout match 1d, 1e and 1q
  after two small style fixes. Next is phase 4, key dates and addenda.

⏺ Phase 4 is built and committed on the same branches, but not deployed. Each
  opportunity now shows its key dates and the next deadline, the questions TODD
  would ask the buyer, and what any addendum changed. As before, it has only run
  against test data, not a real RFP or a real addendum email.

  What it does
  - Next deadline banner at the top of the opportunity page, for example
    "Questions due in 2 days · send to procurement@kingcounty.gov". It's yellow
    when the deadline is within 3 days. Add to calendar downloads a calendar
    (.ics) file with a reminder the day before.
  - Date line: past dates filled in, the next one in blue, the rest grey. A date
    that moved shows the old one struck through. + Add a date covers anything
    the RFP didn't state. If TODD finds no questions deadline, it says so.
  - Questions I'd ask King County:
    - TODD suggests up to 5 questions, each with a short reason like "unclear
      scope" or "finds the incumbent". It picks 3, and you can untick, retick or
      edit any of them.
    - Send N from you@… sends them from your own connected inbox, signed with
      your name and company. Copy for the portal copies them as a numbered list
      instead.
    - Afterwards the card says what was sent, when, and to whom. Once the
      questions deadline passes, the send option goes away.
  - Addenda from the inbox TODD already watches:
    - An addendum or Q&A email for an open RFP is matched by solicitation
      number, or by title when it comes from the same portal. It's no longer
      mistaken for a new RFP.
    - The pink card says, for example, "Addendum 2 arrived. Three things
      changed.", with one line per change. A moved due date updates the date on
      the opportunity.
    - A changed requirement goes back to "not answered" in the compliance
      matrix, marked Check again, and TODD checks it against your draft again. A
      new form joins the checklist.
    - The card links to the items to check, links to the addendum itself, and
      has Mark as read.
  - Right rail: the buyer's answers once they're posted, or "Not posted yet"
    with when to expect them. Useful answers are saved to Knowledge. Below that
    are addenda you've already read and a "Must-haves: all met" line.
  - Cards and Home: the next-date chip from 1h and a "New addendum" tag. Home's
    "Needs you" now lists new addenda and questions due within 3 days.

  Things to know
  - TODD reads only the email text of an addendum. The mailbox code it uses is
    shared with Outreach and doesn't pass attachments through, so I didn't
    change it. When the details are only in the attached PDF, the card says so
    and links to the addendum if the email has a link.
  - One extra AI call per RFP for the dates and questions. It uses the same
    cheaper model as RFP intake, and each matched addendum is one more small
    call.
  - The sign-off on sent questions comes from your profile.
  - I fixed a trap in the gate tests. Without stubs for the new date and
    addendum calls, they reached the production API, and the 401 made the app
    block the test's clicks. They're stubbed now, and I've noted this for future
    specs.
  - Pinned phone bar: on a phone, the pinned action bar for a proposal in
    drafting has three buttons and takes a lot of the screen. That's not new in
    this phase; I can tighten it during the iPhone phase.

  Where it is
  - Web: commit 062c802.
  - Backend: commit d05cf6fb. It adds seven routes and changes the inbox sync,
    so deploy the backend first again.

  Tests
  - Backend: 88 Docs tests pass, including 11 new ones.
  - Web: 71 unit tests and 66 Cypress tests pass, including 4 new Cypress tests.

  Screenshots match 1f and 1g on desktop, and the phone view stacks the same
  content.

  Next is phase 5, the evaluator score-check.

       6 +/** What the assistant does with an ask: show a Mission, show a
         +n answer, or carry on as before. */                            
       7 +export type AssistantRoute = { kind: 'mission'; missionId: stri
         +ng } | { kind: 'answer'; text: string } | null;                
       8 +                                                               
       9 +/**                                                            
      10 + * The TODD assistant's Missions gate, the same in every app (f
         +irst built in                                                  
      11 + * Outreach, 2026-10-20). Real work becomes a Mission shown rig
         +ht in the                                                      
      12 + * conversation; a quick question is answered by the right tool
         +; help                                                         
      13 + * questions (no tool) and any error return null so the app's a
         +ssistant                                                       
      14 + * answers as before. The app's own intercepts (navigation, com
         +posers) run                                                    
      15 + * first. Synced from taliferro-ui/missions - edit the .tmpl th
         +ere.                                                           
      16 + */                                                            
      17 +@Injectable( { providedIn: 'root' } )                          
      18 +export class AssistantMissionsService {                        
      19 +  private readonly api = inject( MissionsApiService );         
      20 +                                                               
      21 +  async route ( request: string, app: string, page?: unknown ):
         + Promise<AssistantRoute> {                                     
      22 +    try {                                                      
      23 +      const res: any = await this.api.ask( request, { origin: '
         +ask', context: this.context( app, page ) } );                  
      24 +      if ( res?.mode === 'mission' && res.mission?.id ) return 
         +{ kind: 'mission', missionId: res.mission.id };                
      25 +      if ( res?.tool && res?.summary ) return { kind: 'answer',
         + text: String( res.summary ) };                                
      26 +      return null;                                             
      27 +    } catch {                                                  
      28 +      return null;                                             
      29 +    }                                                          
      30 +  }

⏺ Phase 5, the score-check, is built and committed on the same branches, but not
  deployed. TODD now estimates how evaluators would score the draft against the
  RFP's own points, and offers a fix for each weak spot. It has only run
  against test data, not a real RFP.

  What it does
  - On Review & send (1i): the first time you open it, TODD scores the draft.
    You see "About 36 of 55", each criterion with a colored bar and one line on
    why it lost points, and the fix with a Fix this button. Run again rescores,
    and the card says when the draft has changed since the last run. It's advice
    only: it never blocks Send.
  - Fix this: opens the editor's new Score tab (1j), with fixes ranked by points
    gained, for example "+6 Add a migration plan" and "Doing both brings it to
    about 45". The revision appears in the draft: the old paragraph struck
    through in pink, the new one in green with +3 points, and Accept, Edit and
    Discard. Accepting rewrites that paragraph, or adds one when the section was
    empty, and then rescores.
  - Section list: shows points per section, for example "6/10".
  - Executive summary check: TODD also checks that it opens with the buyer's
    problem rather than the company's history, and offers a fix if not.
  - Approvers (1n): their link shows "TODD's score-check · About 82 / 100" with
    the points per criterion. They don't see the fixes.

  Things to know
  - Scoring points come from the alert, or from the RFP. If the alert listed the
    points, TODD uses those. Otherwise it reads them from the RFP once. If
    neither has points, the card says there's nothing to score against rather
    than guessing.
  - Each run is one AI call, and each Fix this is one small call. Review & send
    runs it only the first time it opens; after that it's Run again, or
    automatic after you accept a fix.
  - Accepting a fix won't overwrite your own edits. It only replaces a paragraph
    if that paragraph still says what TODD revised; otherwise it adds the new
    paragraph. After you've edited in the document editor, it tells you to copy
    the paragraph across, as before.
  - Replanning starts the score-check over, the same as it does for the
    requirements.
  - There are uncommitted changes in the web repo that aren't mine: the
    assistant box and a new missions folder. I left them alone. If they're from
    the other Claude session, it's now working in this repo too, not only on the
    backend.

  Where it is
  - Web: commit a85cdc2.
  - Backend: commit df6845bc. It adds four routes and puts the score totals on
    the approver's link. Deploy the backend first again.

  Tests
  - Backend: 95 Docs tests pass, including 7 new ones.
  - Web: 75 unit tests and 68 Cypress tests pass, including 2 new Cypress tests.

  Screenshots match 1i, 1j and 1r.

  Next is phase 6, after submittal: clarifications, orals prep, award and
  debrief.

  fix the test by building dates from the local date instead.

⏺ Phase 6, after submittal, is built and committed on the same branches, but not
  deployed. Once a proposal is sent, its page now shows what happens next, from
  the buyer's questions through to the award and debrief. It has only run
  against test data.

  What it does
  - Five stage tiles (1k): Submitted · Clarification · Oral presentation · Award
    · Won/Lost, with dates and the current one in blue. On a phone they scroll
    sideways (1s). This replaces the dates and questions view, which matters
    only before sending.
  - Clarifications (right rail): you paste a question with Log a question, or
    TODD catches it in the inbox it already watches. TODD drafts the answer from
    the proposal you sent and Knowledge, without changing price or commitments.
    You edit it and Send from you@…. It then shows "Everett asked" / "You sent
    · drafted by TODD".
  - Oral presentation: "Invited to an oral presentation? Add the date" takes the
    date, time, length, who's presenting and where, plus Add to calendar. Make
    the prep sheet gives you likely questions (from the score-check's weak
    spots), your 3 win themes and the numbers to know, with Download prep sheet
    as a PDF.
  - Award (1l):
    - We won / We lost / Cancelled. A loss asks why: Price, Experience,
      Technical, Incumbent or Unknown.
    - After a loss, you get a debrief request already filled in (from you, to
      the procurement contact, with a subject), sent from your email. "Remind me
      if there's no reply in 5 business days" puts it in Home's Needs you, and
      They replied clears it.
    - After a win, you can save the winning sections you pick to Knowledge.
  - History: every stage with its date and a note, newest first.
  - Home's Needs you also gets questions to answer, orals within 3 days, and
    debriefs with no reply.

  Things to know
  - Win and loss reasons now feed the fit score. Recent outcomes and their
    reasons go into what TODD reads when it scores a new RFP. That's why the
    award card says "Win and loss reasons go into how I score new RFPs". I left
    out the design's "after 5 outcomes, I'll show which kinds of RFPs you win",
    since nothing does that yet.
  - The iOS app shows Cancelled as Lost. That's how it's stored, so the app
    still reads it; the web shows "Cancelled".
  - I left out Practice with TODD. An interactive mock Q&A is a feature of its
    own; tell me if you want it.
  - What TODD reads from an inbox clarification: the email's text, the same
    limit as addenda. The inbox pass now also watches submitted proposals.
  - I removed the old "We won" / "We lost" buttons from the right rail; the
    award card replaces them. The rail now shows "Sent Oct 12" instead of the
    RFP's closing date.
  - I fixed a test that broke on the date. The key-dates spec counted days in
    UTC, so it failed once UTC had rolled past midnight.

  Where it is
  - Web: commit 065ce8e.
  - Backend: commit 505ffbb8. It adds nine routes, so deploy the backend first
    again.

  Tests
  - Backend: 103 Docs tests pass, including 8 new ones.
  - Web: 80 unit tests and 72 Cypress tests pass, including 4 new Cypress tests.

  Screenshots match 1k, 1l and 1s.

  What's left is phase 7, the approval-link screens 1m and 1n. They're mostly
  built already, and the score-check is on the approver page now. Then phase 8,
  the iPad and iPhone pass, which includes fixing the tall pinned action bar on
  phones.

⏺ Phase 7 is built and committed on the same branches, but not deployed. Most of
  the approval-link flow already existed, so this phase brings Review & send
  and the approver's page in line with 1m and 1n.

  What changed
  - Summary line on Review & send: under "1 of 3 approved." it now says what
    they're approving, for example "Version 3 · 24 pages · checklist complete ·
    score-check about 82 of 100. If you edit the proposal, approvals start
    over."
  - Approvers card (1m): moved from the main column to the right rail. Each
    person shows:
    - their role, for example "Dana Lee · Partner";
    - where they are: "Approved v3 · 2:14 PM", "Opened 1:50 PM · not decided" or
      "Link copied · not opened";
    - Copy link. The ⋯ menu still offers your mail app or a text message.
  - Under the approvers: "Add an approver by name" (for example "Marcus Reed,
    Accountant", or an email address), Copy all links (one line per person who
    still has to approve), Share… (the phone's share sheet, or copy where there
    isn't one), and the "Let approvers suggest edits to the text" switch.
  - Approver's page (1n): reads "Reviewing as Marcus Reed", then "Proposal ·
    version 3 — Tyrone wants your OK to send this to Pierce County. This is
    exactly what will be sent before Oct 24." Download PDF gives them the exact
    version. It was on the not-built list from the approval round; it builds the
    PDF the same way Review & send does. The score-check card from phase 5 is
    there too.
  - Short link: /a/{token} now opens the same page. The links TODD makes stay
    /approve/…, so links you've already shared keep working.

  Things to know
  - Roles are optional. A name alone still works, and the approval-request
    dialog is unchanged; you add roles from the card.
  - The approvers card replaces the status tags, so I updated the existing
    approval test's checks to read the new status lines.
  - The /a/ link needed one fix. It's written so the token comes through when
    the dev server renders the page, which a plain redirect didn't do.

  Where it is
  - Web: commit c4b9392.
  - Backend: commit a198e619, which adds a role for each approver.

  Tests
  - Backend: all 19 approval tests pass, including 1 new one.
  - Web: 83 unit tests and 74 Cypress tests pass, including 2 new Cypress tests.

  The last phase is phase 8, iPad and iPhone (1o, 1p–1s). It includes making the
  pinned action bar on phones shorter, which has come up a few times.

⏺ All 8 phases of the lifecycle handoff are now built and committed. None of it
  is deployed, and none of it has run against a real RFP or a real mailbox yet;
  every test uses test data.

  What changed in this last phase (iPhone and iPad)
  - Opportunity page on a phone (1p, 1s):
    - A new RFP opens on its must-haves, with "1 met · 2 missing · 1 to confirm"
      at a glance. The dates come after.
    - The stage line is one row that scrolls sideways instead of wrapping onto
      two lines.
    - The pinned bar holds only the main action and one row of two buttons, for
      example "We can't meet this" with "Draft anyway" and "Read the RFP", or
      "Open the proposal" with "Review & send" and "Read the RFP". The status
      line is gone from the bar, since the stage line already shows it. That
      fixes the tall pinned bar from earlier phases.
  - Review & send on a phone: the Approvers card sits right under "1 of 2
    approved." instead of at the very bottom.
  - Proposal editor on a phone: keeps its Review & send button. It used to be
    hidden, which left no direct way to Review & send from the editor on a
    phone.
  - iPad (1o): the two-column layout already matched. The must-haves header now
    shows "2 missing" at every size, as on iPad.

  Tests
  - Web: 83 unit tests and 76 Cypress tests pass, including a new phone and iPad
    layout spec.
  - Backend: 104 Docs tests pass across the lifecycle phases.

  What you now have
  - Phases 1–2: a status line, and the Bid / No-Bid gate (must-haves, "We can't
    meet this", Draft anyway with a reason).
  - Phase 3: the compliance matrix: requirements, Write this, export as an
    appendix, and Send blocked until everything is answered.
  - Phase 4: key dates, questions for the buyer sent from your email, and
    addenda and buyer answers read from your inbox.
  - Phase 5: the evaluator score-check, with Fix this.
  - Phase 6: after submittal: clarifications, orals and the prep sheet, the
    award, the debrief, and win and loss reasons that feed the fit score.
  - Phase 7: approval by private link, as in 1m and 1n.
  - Phase 8: the phone and iPad layout.

  To ship it
  - Web: branch docs-lifecycle (latest commit 1707efd).
  - Backend: branch docs-lifecycle in the worktree at
    /Users/tyshowers/Dropbox/corporate/taliferrotech-lifecycle. It contains a
    todd symlink to the credentials, which must not be committed.
  - Order: deploy the backend first, then the web. The web needs the new routes.

  Left out on purpose:
  - Practice with TODD from 1k.
  - The 1h row list with filters: I kept the card grid and added the new tags.
  - Reading addendum attachments: the mailbox code shared with Outreach drops
    them, so TODD reads only the email text.

  Any of these can come next if you want them.

  I've already done the hard part of that.

  Web app (Docs): your deploys run from the feature branch. deploy.sh builds,
  runs the tests, deploys todd-docs and pushes whatever branch you're on; the
  Oct 19 deploy went out from proposal-approval. docs-lifecycle already contains
  proposal-approval and your "updates to docs" commit. main is just behind and
  doesn't block anything; it fast-forwards cleanly if you want it tidy.

  Backend: this one needs a merge. Production deploys come from work-in-progress
  in your main checkout. My work is on docs-lifecycle in the separate worktree,
  and it carries the approval-links backend as well. That backend is not on
  work-in-progress yet.

  may be calling backend routes that were never deployed. This deploy fixes that
